LETTER OF TRANSMITTAL SELECT COMMITTEE ON SMALL BUSINESS OF THE HOUSE OF REPRESENTATIVES OF THE UNITED STATES, 

Washington, D.C., November 8, 1962.

To Members of the House Small Business Committee: 

The accompanying staff report, entitled "Mergers and Superconcentration: Acquisitions of 500 Largest Industrial and 50 Largest

Merchandising Firms," has been prepared at my request. The report presents voluminous data on merger actions of large firms during the 11-year period since the passage of the Celler-Kefauver Act, and thus affords an insight into the impact on small business of the merger movement.

America is at the crossroads. It must face up to the alternative paths lying ahead. Will it follow the road to monopoly and everincreasing concentration of economic power? Or will it take the other alternative-the hard and rocky road to a restoration of competitive free enterprise, with the preservation of the American ideals of opportunities for small, locally oriented, companies to enter business and thrive and grow?

The Congress has repeatedly paid allegiance to a competitive enterprise system. Such allegiance has been expressed in the passage of our various key antitrust statutes the Sherman Act, the Clayton

Act, the Federal Trade Commission Act, the Robinson-Patman Act, and the Celler-Kefauver Act. Both the Senate and the House have 

Small Business Committees, and small business has a voice in the executive branch in the Small Business Administration. 

Yet despite these noble efforts designed to preserve opportunity for small, local enterprise, big business has grown apace. Loopholes have appeared in our antitrust laws, and big business has fashioned new techniques of controlling markets and enhancing its power. 

It is most appropriate that this report be titled "Mergers and Superconcentration," for all appearances suggest that we are moving into a new phase of industrial and financial domination and control of American industry. Merger movements have fed this cancerous growth. The United States is rapidly becoming a nation of clerks and hired hands. Opportunities for independent small businesses are eroding.

More than a decade has elapsed since the Celler-Kefauver Act was enacted with the purpose of forestalling increasing concentration resulting from mergers and acquisitions. By interesting coincidence, on the very day (June 25, 1962) that the Supreme Court handed down its first opinion (Brown Shoe Co. v. United States) interpreting the 

Celler-Kefauver Act of 1950, the Senate Antitrust Subcommittee, III 

1621526

IV LETTER OF TRANSMITTAL 

under the chairmanship of Senator Kefauver, issued a new report on manufacturing concentration. According to Senator Kefauver- 

The most important single fact brought out by the report is that in the years following World War II the industrial economy has become significantly more concentrated in the hands of a few large corporations. Thus, between 1947 and 

1958 the share held by the 200 largest companies of total industrial output, as measured by value added by manufacture, rose 27 percent. In 1947 the 200 largest companies accounted for 30 percent of the value added by manufacture; by 1958 their proportion had risen to 38 percent. 

The present report on "Mergers and Superconcentration" indicates that much of the recent increase in concentration has resulted from merger activity of the larger corporations. It shows that, based on the information reported in the various financial manuals, the top 500 industrial firms absorbed some 3,404 other companies and the 50 largest merchandising firms acquired 332 companies. Moreover, based on limited data as to the size of the acquired firms, the preponderance of the assets removed from the stream of private business enterprise has stemmed from merger actions of the top corporations. 

This report, therefore, deserves careful study and presents a clear warning.

The merger movement has, of course, affected many lines of business in addition to those in which the large industrial and merchandising firms are engaged. In particular, it has made enormous inroads in the field of commercial banking-traditionally an important small business field in the United States. The latest annual report of the 

Federal Deposit Insurance Corporation, for example, indicates that in 1961 some 133 banks, operating some 423 banking offices, with aggregate resources of $5,994 million, were absorbed by mergers, consolidations, and acquisitions.2 

I wish to express the committee's gratitude to the Federal Trade

Commission and its Bureau of Economics for supplying to us the merger data for this report, based on the Commission's regular merger investigations. Thanks are also extended to the National Industrial 

Conference Board for granting permission to reproduce comprehensive information on merger actions brought by the antitrust agencies. 

For the committee, this project was under the direction of Harrison F. Houghton, chief economist, assisted by Miss Adele Jaworowski. 

Sincerely yours,

WRIGHT PATMAN, Chairman, Select Committee on Small Business. 

1 Report prepared by Bureau of the Census for Antitrust and Monopoly Subcommittee of the Senate 

Judiciary Committee, "Concentration Ratios in Manufacturing Industry, 1958," pt. I. 

2 Annual report of the Federal Deposit Insurance Corporation, 1961, p. 11. The House Small Business 

Committee has underway a number of inquiries into banking, including regional concentration of commercial banking, growth of chain, holding company, and branch banking, the bank merger movement and its impact on concentration in banking, availability of bank credit for small business, and related subjects.

This is the conclusion of the Wall Street Journal in regard to recent merger actions of food processors and food chain retailers;ontact underbeque visits Tot "The two expansion trends among food processors and many retailers reinforce each other. Bigger retail chains and cooperative purchasing by smaller grocers result in a greater amount of bulk buying. 

This in turn requires larger processing organizations, and leads to predictions in the industry that small local and regional companies will sell less and less of the Nation's food in the future. "Behind the urge to merge is the profit motive. Although food processors had record sales last year and expect volume this year to rise $2 billion to $80 billion, their average rate of profit on sales has been on the wane. This rate slipped to 3.2 percent last year from 4.6 percent in 1939. "To maintain or increase earnings in the face of this slump, processors need greater volume. Wherever possible, they've used the short-cut of taking over established businesses to get it." [Emphasis added.] "Food 

Processors, Retailers Seek Non-Food Merger to Lift Margins," Wall Street Journal, Aug. 1, 1962.

Are our present antitrust laws adequate to meet the emerging pattern of market structure change following in the train of the merger movement? Are such laws sufficient to cope with the ever-growing merger movement and establishment of huge conglomerate corporations? Should we take a new look at the merger movement to study its economic and social implications?

SCOPE OF STUDY 

The present report attempts to provide the "raw material" for such a study. It focuses attention on the merger actions of the top corporations during the past decade in the hope of shedding some light on these many questions. It presents a listing of reported acquisitions of the 500 largest industrial corporations and the 50 largest merchandising firms. The list of top industrial and merchandising firms was made by Fortune magazine. The merger listings were compiled by the Federal Trade Commission. The period selected for review covers the years 1951 through 1961. This 11-year period is of interest for two reasons. First, it marks the period between the passage of the Celler-Kefauver Act of 1950 and the first Supreme Court decision interpreting that act. Second, it covers the second cycle of merger activity during the period since World War II-a cycle which commenced in 1950 and has continued to rise unabated since that time, carrying mergers to one of the highest levels on record. 4 5 To provide perspective as to the relationship between antitrust activity and the .nerger movement, the report includes information concerning each of the actions taken by the Federal Trade Commission and the Antitrust Division under the Celler-Kefauver Act." 

Also, the report analyzes the merger movement in relation to recent changes in business population, reviews past merger movements and their influence on present business structure and concentration, and reviews the pattern of recent antitrust enforcement and the characteristics of the merger actions of the top corporations during the recent wave of mergers.

In the Bethlehem-Youngstown steel merger case, successfully prosecuted by the Antitrust Division, a group of small businessmen producing wire rope faced an economic squeeze. Youngstown represented one of the few remaining noncompeting sources of supply for rope wire, the raw material out of which wire rope is fabricated. Moreover, Youngstown, an important factor in the oilfield supply business, was also an important customer for small fabricators of wire rope. Bethlehem, on the other hand, did not sell rope wire to the small independent wire rope fabricators. It was in the business of producing and selling wire rope itself.

Thus, the small business independent wire rope fabricators faced the prospect of losing an important independent, noncompeting source of supply for their raw material, rope wire, and at the same time an important customer for their wire rope. This was a serious squeeze that the merger would have placed on the independent wire rope producers.

These independent wire rope producers had a delicate source-customer relationship with Youngstown. 

Moreover, they testifled that wire rope prices were highly competitive, but that rope wire prices, controlled by the integrated steel producers, were rigid and noncompetitive. United States v. Bethlehem, 168 F. Supp. 

576 (S.D. N.Y, 1958). 

4 15 U.S.C. 18 and 21.

Brown Shoe Co. v. U.S., 370 U.S. 294 (1962). 

These are included as appendix tables A-C.

The Select Committee on Small Business of the House is greatly concerned with barriers to the entry, growth, and survival of small business. This report of the merger movement as it affects manufacturing and trade is one part of a broader study in this area. Another intensive inquiry is in progress in the field of commercial banking—an area of great importance to small business, both because independent banking is a leading small business sector in its own right, but, even more significant, because the availability of commercial bank credit is crucial to the survival of small business in all fields.

CHAPTER THE POSTWAR MERGER MOVEMENT AND CHANGES IN BUSINESS POPULATION 

The merger movement is one important index of the disappearance of small business from the stream of competitive enterprise. Characteristically, acquisitions are made of firms which have progressed well beyond the initial years of operation. They have usually established themselves in a particular line of activity. They have, more often than not, experienced a degree of success. When such firms disappear there is a significant loss to the ranks of independent small business.

The question may be asked, however, whether the disappearance of small business by merger is offset to some extent by the entry of other firms. Thus, it is informative to consider the postwar merger movement within the context of trends in the business population. 

The present study of mergers is confined to those made by the largest industrial and merchandising firms. Thus, the most relevant business population data to consider would be those in these two broad business categories.

SMALL VERSUS LARGE FIRMS 

First, however, a word about the relative importance of small business in manufacturing and retail trade. "Small business" is, of course, a relative term. A small firm in one industry may have several thousand employees, while a large firm in another field may have relatively few employees. According to the Department of 

Commerce: 

*** it has been the practice for each investigator to select a definition he considers suited to the needs of the particular purposes at hand. A "large" filling station obviously has fewer employees than a "small" steel mill, and the same employment criterion cannot be used to isolate the large firms in both industries.2 

Although there are, of course, other ways of determining size,³ the number of paid employees is probably the most useful and convenient measure. A fixed standard of 500 employees is often used as a cutoff point between large and small business, but while this may be a fairly accurate broad gage for all industry, it is less applicable to particular fields.* 

1 The latest semiannual report of the Small Business Administration carries a section devoted to "Defining 'Small Business," and points out that "one of the Small Business Administration's basic responsibilities is to determine which firms within an industry or field of operation are 'small businesses' for the purpose of obtaining financial, Government contracts, and other assistance from the agency.' 

The agency has set up a Size Policy Committee to assist in establishing policies on size standards for each 

SBA program. See 17th Semiannual Report, Small Business Administration, July 1-Dec. 31, 1961, pp. 

61, 62. 

2 U.S. Department of Commerce, Survey of Current Business, September 1959, p. 18. 

"Total assets, fixed investment, sales, and income are other common indicators of size, and for some purposes these financial variables may be of more interest than the number of paid employees." Ibid., p. 17.

For example, the average firm in the top 1 percent of the firms in all industry in 1956 had 560 employees. 

The corresponding average for manufacturing was 3,200 employees and for retail trade, 180 employees. Ibid., ., p. 17.

The accompanying table 1 shows a percentage distribution of firms, according to employment size, for retail trade, manufacturing, and all industry—in terms of (1) number of firms, and (2) total employment.

It is apparent that the overwhelming percentage of the number of firms would be classified as "small business." Thus, for all industries, 99.8 percent of all firms have less than 500 employees, and 99.1 percent have fewer than 100 employees. In the case of retail trade, 99.9 percent of the firms have fewer than 100 employees, and 97.7 percent have fewer than 20 employees.

The average size of firms is much larger in manufacturing than in retail trade or industry as a whole. Some 76.2 percent of the manufacturing firms have fewer than 20 employees; 98.8 percent have less than 500 employees.

Total employment, on the other hand, is concentrated in the large firms, particularly in manufacturing. About 60 percent of the manufacturing employees work in firms with 500 or more employees. In retail trade, one-fourth of the employees work in firms with 500 or more employees, while about 40 percent are employed in firms with 

50 or more workers.

These figures demonstrate that small firms, particularly in retail trade, dominate the number of firms in the business population, but that employment is concentrated in the large firms, especially in manufacturing.

The data refer to 1956, the latest available. According to the Department of Commerce, however. "postwar experience has shown that relative distributions of firms and their employment by size of firm tend to change slowly and present patterns are probably very similar to those found for 1956." Ibid., p. 14.

POSTWAR CHANGES IN BUSINESS POPULATION 

The merger movement thus may be considered within the context of postwar trends in the business population. 

During the immediate postwar years the number of businesses showed a sharp increase. In fact, the year 1946, the first full year after the conclusion of World War II, marked the inauguration of the largest number of new business for any year since 1940. Also, the immediate postwar years witnessed a low level of business discontinuances.

The growth of the business population, however, has tapered off considerably in the past decade, a period which has coincided with a new surge in the merger movement.

This declining growth in the business population is described by the Department of Commerce as follows: 6 

The number of operating concerns at the beginning of 1962 reached 44 million, increasing about 40,000 firms, or somewhat less than 1 percent, during 1961. 

This rise was slightly smaller than that for 1960, with both years affected by lessened business activity during parts of the year * * * In the past decade the total business population has risen steadily at a rate of slightly under 12 percent per year. This growth in the number of firms slackened in the past 2 years to about 1 percent per annum. The accompanying chart (chart 1) shows the postwar trends in the total business population for all industries and for manufacturing, in comparison to gross national product (in constant dollars). It may be seen that the "All industry" business population figures have moved ahead steadily, but at a declining rate in comparison to the growth in gross national product.

The number of businesses operating in the field of manufacturing increased sharply for about 3 years after the war, showed little further growth from then until 1959, and since then the number of manufacturing concerns has actually dropped to the lowest level since 1948. 

Tables 2 and 3 provide a comparison of changes in business population, by broad industry groupings, with changes in gross national product during the past decade. Over this interval the growth rate of the business population has been 1.5 percent per year, while that for gross national product was 3.1 percent. The number of retail firms has risen at the rate of 1.0 percent per year, while the manufacturing business population has declined at a rate of 0.3 percent per year. 

U.S. Department of Commerce, Survey of Current Business, June 1962, pp. 11, 24. 

Data for chart 1 are shown in app. D.

SIGNIFICANCE TO BUSINESS STRUCTURE OF DISAPPEARANCES BY MERGER 

evede f

As indicated above, disappearances by merger have a much moresignificant relationship to the structure of business organization thảm do disappearances in the characteristic "entry, exit" sense. Acquisi tions usually are of going concerns, well established in their particular lines of business. They may be large, medium sized, or small, but in any case, represent a valued asset to the acquiring firm. New entrants, on the other hand, characteristically are small, marginal firms. 

Their chances of survival are not great, and most discontinued businesses are not only small ones, but recent entrants. As pointed out by the Department of Commerce: 

Business turnover rates that is, the ratio of the number of new, discontinued or transferred businesses to the number of firms in operation are useful guides in assessing industry differentials in the stability of the business population and 

Sensitivity to changes 

Past studies of the business popula rates correlated with type of activity, with geographical area, with legal form of organization, and particularly with size of form. The artendency of entry rates to be high among small firms and low among larger firms 

tion have found turno business activity.

Thus, it is clear that disappearances of independent enterprises are not easily offset by new entrants. "Indeed, it is in the expansion and growth of enterprises that have already weathered risks of the early years that the greatest "entry" challenge to dominant firms lies, and these are likely to be the very firms that disappear through mergers.

The current merger movement, which comenced during the closing years of World War II, is one of three major merger movements in recent American history. The first took place in the 1890's and the early years of this century; the second occurred in the 1920's. 

The post World War II merger movement, as indicated in the accompanying chart (chart 2), occurred in two cycles. The first extended from about 1943 to high points in 1946 and 1947, and then tapered off. The second cycle began in 1950 and continued to a higher peak in 1961. There is no indication of a diminution in the upward sweep of the present merger movement.

The characteristics of the three major merger movements have been succinctly described by two outstanding studies-one by a leading independent research organization, and the other by the 

Federal Trade Commission. 

BYNASTY CHAPTER 3 THE THREE MERGER MOVEMENTS OF THE TWENTIETH CENTURY THE MERGER MOVEMENT OF THE 1890's 

The first major merger movement has been characterized in the following words:

The first recorded merger movement of major proportions occurred as the United States entered the twentieth century, its peak years being 1898 through 1902. 

In many respects it was the most important of the major merger waves. It transformed many industries, formerly characterized by many small- and mediumsized firms, into those in which one or a few very large enterprises occupied leading positions. It laid the foundation for the industriál structure that has characterized most of American industry in the twentieth century.2 

8 230AR. * ** The principal objective of this movement was to gain control of the market, not only through horizontal combinations of competitors, but also through combining into a vertical structure all of the stages of production from raw materials to finished goods. (For example, the United States Steel Corp. brought under one control not only the greater part of the iron and steel producing and rolling facilities, but also organizations extracting and transporting iron ore, coal, and other raw materials, from the ground up.) Among the present-day industrial giants which emerged during that period as great consolidations were: 

American Tobacco Co. (1890), American Sugar Refining Co. (1891), General 

Electric Co. (1892), United States Rubber Co. (1892), United States Leather

Co. (1893), Pittsburgh Plate Glass Co. (1895), New Jersey Zinc Co. (1897), 

National Biscuit Co. (1898), Amalgamated Copper Co. (1899), American Car & Foundry Co. (1899), American Woolen Co. (1899), International Paper Co. (1899), Pullman Co. (1899), International Silver Co. (1899), United Shoe Machinery Corp. (1899), American_Brass Co. (1900), United States Steel Corp. (1901), American Can Co. (1901), Eastman Kodak Co. THE MERGER MOVEMENT OF THE 1920's 

The merger movement of the 1920's has been described as follows: It

The second large movement took place in the years 1926 through 1930. reflected to some degree the emergence of new leading industries in the years since the first merger wave. To some degree it represented attempts to restore the industrial concentration achieved by the first merger wave, a concentration which had become diluted over the years.¹ During the twenties, external expansion through the merger movement again reached a peak, the typical action generally involving one of the "leaders" of a major industry. Thus, many of the horizontal mergers were engineered by concerns, which, though not the largest in the industry, were still among its first three or four (e.g., in steel, Bethlehem Steel Corp. and Republic Steel Corp.; and in chemicals, Allied Chemical & Dye). Vertical expansions took on the character of intervention into medium-sized business sectors producing parts, components, etc., consumed in the mass-production industries (e.g., in the automobile industry), while the encroachment of large corporations into the fabricating fields (e.g., the steel and copper industries) was promoted to a limited extent. In some characteristically small-business industries, which had been relatively untouched by mergers, new consolidations were formed and expanded to great prominence by the acquisition route (e.g., National Dairy Products, General Foods, and Standard Brands).5 

THE POST-WORLD WAR II MERGER MOVEMENT 

The early phases of the current merger movement have been described as follows: The third movement, a product of the decade following World War II, differs from the two earlier merger waves, having a lower peak and a wider spread across most of the postwar decade. ***It was thus not so clearly a sharp burst of business reorganization as were the two earlier movements, whose greatest activity was bunched in 5 successive years. Both in the absolute volume of merger activity and in its size relative to the business population, this latest merger wave is clearly smaller than the first two. Nor is it clear that the latest merger movement has had comparable effects upon the level of concentration." In appraising the overall effect of mergers on economic concentration, it must be constantly borne in mind that they tend to become cumulative over a period of time. In other words, each year's mergers are superimposed upon a structure of economic concentration which has been built up over many past years.7 The importance of external expansion in promoting concentration has never been more clearly revealed than in the acquisition movement that is taking place at the present time a movement which is strengthening the position of big business in several ways. In the first place, several of the traditionally "smallbusiness" industries have been affected. The two leading industries, in terms of number of acquisitions, have been textiles and apparel, and food and kindred

CHANGES IN CONCENTRATION 

Mergers bear an important relationship to changes in concentration. 

Commenting on the role of mergers in the growth of large firms, one economist states:

The factual data of this and other studies on mergers suggest that present concentration would be smaller if many early mergers had been prevented." 

He adds:

Existing levels of industrial concentration are due almost entirely to external growth.10 

He goes on to point out that:

The combination movement at the turn of the century resulted in a high degree of concentration in a large number of industries. The subsequent growth of assets and sales in these industries was extremely great. This explains why external growth represents only a small fraction of the present absolute size of the dominant firms in oligopolistic industries." [Emphasis added.] 

Another economist states: 

It has been fairly conclusively established that concentration of control of assets increased between 1924 and 1929, the 6-year span that includes most of the merger activity associated with the second merger movement.12 

Concentration may be measured in various ways. For example, it may be gaged in terms of specific lines of commerce in particular geographic markets. It may also be measured in terms of products or industries on regional or national bases. Finally, it may be measured in terms of position in the total economy or some major segment of the economy occupied by, say, the 100 largest or the 200 largest firms. beTwo recent studies indicate that the position of the largest firms in our economy has risen during the post-World War II years to reach the highest levels of the past half century. mah nah g 

8 Ibid., pp. 25-28.

J. Fred Weston, "The Role of Mergers in the Growth of Large Firms" (University of California Press, Berkeley, 1953), p. 92. 

10 Ibid., p. 102. 

11 Ibid. 

12 Jesse W. Markham, "Survey of the Evidence and Findings on Mergers," in "Business Concentration and Price Policy," National Bureau of Economic Research (Princeton, 1955), p. 171 fn.

In retrospect, it may well be that had section 7 of the Clayton Act been an effective law during the 1920's and 1930's, concentration in the steel and other industries would have declined. For example, United 

States Steel's position held constant during the twenties and declined in the thirties. The rise in industry concentration during the twenties stemmed from mergers among other "top tier" companies (notably Bethlehem and Republic). United States Steel had 40 percent of the industry capacity for steel ingots both in 1920 and 1930. Since that time its position has declined to below 30 percent. From 1920 to 1930, however, the position of the next 3 steel companies rose from 6.3 to 14.2 percent, and the "big 4" combined rose from 

54,5 to 67.2 percent. In 1958, United States Steel had 28.6 percent, the next 3 had 30.3 percent and the "big 4" combined, 58.9 percent. Consummation of the Bethlehem-Youngstown merger would have boosted the "big 4" to 63.5 percent of total industry capacity. Cf. U.S. v. Bethlehem, Findings of Fact and Conclusions of Law, pp. 18 and 42.

A similar situation occurred in the tin can manufacturing business. The leading producer was formed at the beginning of the century as a consolidation of 125 independents, with about 85 percent of the business under its control. A little more than a decade later its position had dropped to slightly above 50 percent: it now has about 42 percent of the business. However, the second largest company entered a vast program of acquisitions during the twenties which is still continuing, and the "big 2" now share over 70 percent of the business! Cf. Weston, op. cit., p. 41; "Corporate and Bank Mergers," Interim Report of the Antitrust 

Subcommittee on the Judiciary, House of Representatives, 84th Cong. 1st sess., pursuant to H. Res. 22, 1955, p. 7.

The most recent study is that issued by the Senate Subcommittee on Antitrust and Monopoly. CHAPTER PATTERNS OF THE CURRENT MERGER MOVEMENT 

A review of mergers and acquisitions undertaken by the top corporations is of major interest in indicating the patterns of the current merger movement. Is big business expanding horizontally, vertically, or in conglomerate fashion? These broad questions suggest certain more detailed queries. Horizontal mergers are those which bring together members of the same line of business. The classic examples of such mergers were simple and straightforward. Erstwhile competitors would join forces in an effort to settle a competitive battle between themselves and gain some control of the market. 1 Horizontal mergers, however, may be further subclassified into those involving (a) firms operating in the same geographical market and (b) market extensions of firms into new geographical markets. 

The economic implications of the "market extension" type mergers are somewhat different from the pure horizontal mergers. The latter may eliminate substantial existing competition, while the former could reduce potential competition. Horizontal mergers may involve two additional subcategories, (c) "product extension" or "broadening of lines", and (d) substitute products. Here again the competition affected may be actual or potential. 

Vertical mergers may be prompted either to insure a source of raw material supply or a market outlet. The competitive consequences of vertical mergers are not so clear and obvious as those flowing from pure horizontal mergers. Sometimes vertical mergers are motivated by a desire to gain control of an entire line of supply, extending from the extractive phase through basic manufacturing and fabricating and on through wholesaling and retailing to the ultimate consumer. At other times partial vertical thrusts are attempted in an effort to shake loose domination of a particular stage in the vertical chain. Small business may be adversely affected by vertical mergers by being foreclosed from independent sources of supply or from markets. 

The conglomerate merger, which has been of growing concern in recent years to students of industrial organization, is sometimes explained by the single word "diversification." The purposes and implications of the conglomerate merger,2 however, are usually much more complex than the laudable effort of the forward-looking business executive to avoid putting "all of his eggs in one basket." 

1 In the Foremost Dairy case before the Federal Trade Commission it was argued that "once entry is made by acquisition and a relatively local operation has been replaced by a product- and market-diversified Foremost, a material change in competitive prospectus has transpired from the viewpoint of the remaining competition, particularly that segment which is less favorably situated than Foremost.' Appeal brief of counsel supporting the complaint before the FTC, In the Matter of Foremost Dairies, Inc. (Doc. No. 6495), p. 7. See also, Opinion of the Commission, Foremost Dairies, supra, Apr. 30, 1962, pp. 44-46. 

2 See John M. Blair, "The Conglomerate Merger in Economics and Law," the Georgetown Law Journal, vol. 46, No. 4, Summer 1958, p. 672 et seq. Also Corwin D. Edwards, "Conglomerate Bigness as a Source of Power" (and comment by George W. Stocking), in "Business Concentration and Price Policy," National 

Bureau of Economic Research (Princeton 1955), p. 331 et seq. 1424

The large corporation has an array of advantages which may be exploited in pursuit of conglomerate expansion, including ready access to short-term and long-term credit, discriminations and preferences in buying, control over distribution, etc., which can be used to soften up a potential acquiree. The large corporation can subsidize the sale of products in the newly entered "conglomerate" line with profits made in other phases of its operations. Such a potential threat can be an inducement for small business to "take cover" through quick sale.

Conglomerate mergers may have an effect both on the competitive characteristics and the survival prospects of small business in the industries in which the acquisitions are made. Formerly small business fields may be transformed into big business industries as the smaller firms find it more and more difficult to match the advantages enjoyed by their larger rivals. The nature of competition may similarly be altered, assuming the role of "monopolistic competition" with emphasis on promotion and product differentiation and the subordination of price competition.

These merger categories are, of course, sometimes more conceptual than real, and are affected by dynamic changes in industry organization. Under a simple industry structure, a given merger may be vertical, as a firm in one stage reaches out to another stage. Once the firm has entered the field, the second merger could be both horizontal and vertical. Similarly, initial intervention into a field may represent conglomerate acquisition, but subsequent acquisitions in the same line would be horizontal.

Merger actions are sometimes judged solely by simple profit_motive. 

For example, an amusing article in the May 1962 issue of the Business

Review, published by the Federal Reserve Bank of Philadelphia, contains the following opening paragraphs:

Corporations marry for money. Simon pure, unabashed golddigging is the sole motive behind every business merger. How they meet is a matter of chance or choice. Sometimes the marriage is a culmination of a long and mutually profitable business relationship; in other instances, they may meet on a stratoliner, or a golf course, or at a convention. Then, too, some mergers are arranged by the 

John Aldens of business; some would-be brides even put an ad in the newspapers where they put their best foot forward, so to speak, with the objective—matrimony. 

Corporate mores sanction initial advances by the bride. 

The groom is generally bigger and often, although not always, older, worldlier, and wealthier. Reliable records and ratings on substance and prenuptial debts outstanding are readily available to both parties upon consulting any of the wellknown financial services. Debutantes are not necessarily the most attractive. 

While they may have good corporate configuration and a glint of profit in their eyes, they must compete with their older sisters of demonstrated profitability and good connections in the business world.

Such a description of the merger process takes no account of the public interest or the role of public policy, in relation to the preservation of a competitive system with opportunities for small business. 

In the modern 20th century industrial complex, the impact of the merger movement has many ramifications. The Celler-Kefauver 

Act in 1950 recognized that neither the Sherman Act of 1890-directed against single firm monopoly or combinations and conspiracies-or the original Clayton Act-directed at incipient monopoly-was sufficient to cope with modern merger problems. Thus the 1950 amendment to section 7 of the Clayton Act (1) eliminated the necessity to prove intent; (2) made clear that the scope of the act was broad

01 enough to encompass horizontal, vertical, and conglomerate mergers; and (3) established a market effects criterion, much broader than the question of whether competition had been eliminated between erst while competitors. In other words, in the new Anti-Merger Act 

Congress attempted to face up to the modern world of oligopolies, which by sophisticated methods arrive at "unconversational understandings," and whose merger actions often have cumulative rami fications detrimental to the welfare of small business. Indeed, one of the problems of small business is whether it is suffering from side effects of the merger movement in the form of of new b barriers to entry, expansion, and survival."

JOA SHERMÁN ACT DID NOT CHECK MERGER MOVEMENT OF 1890's G

Efforts to employ the Sherman Act to check the great merger movement of the 1890's foundered on legal niceties. The first antitrust case under the Sherman Act to reach the Supreme C Court a major merger. The American Sugar Refining Co., which already controlled 65 percent of the sugar refined in the United States, merged with three independent refiners, to raise the company's percentage to 98 percent of the industry. Sugar prices were immediately advanced. 

By any test, this was a monopolistic merger. The Supreme Court acknowledged, in fact, that American Sugar Refining Co. had a which involved 2. 2 6492 3643 

Act of July 2, 1890 (c. 647, 26 Stat, 209, 51st Cong., 1st sess. S. 1, Public 190) 22 100 abor! Janabol •

4 monopoly, but decided against the Government on the ground that the Sherman Act applied only to commerce, that sugar refining was manufacturing, and that manufacturing was not commerce. 

The Sugar case was decided in 1895, just 5 years after the Sherman

Act was passed. This was the year that marked the beginning of the turn-of-the-century merger movement, which was, to quote one authority, "a burst of merger activity never exceeded in importance 

In subsequent Sherman Act decisions the Supreme Court ignored the strict interpretation of "commerce" followed in the Sugar case, and the Government succeeded in dissolving the Standard Oil and the tobacco trusts. However, these decisions were handed down in 

1911, several years after the termination of the merger movement which was so influential in setting the pattern of industrial concentration in the American economy.

SECTION 7 OF CLAYTON ACT DID NOT CHECK MERGER MOVEMENT OF 1920's 

Congress made another effort to check the growth of concentration resulting from mergers in 1914 when it passed the Clayton Act. 

This act, however, went even further than the Sherman Act, being designed "to arrest the creation *** of monopolies in their incipiency." Section 7 of the Clayton Act outlawed mergers which "may substantially lessen competition or tend to create a monopoly." 8 

In other words, intervention was to be made before mergers had reached monopoly proportions.

But when this act was brought into play in the 1920's to cope with the second great merger movement it was discovered that a loophole in the law made it a nullity. The Clayton Act restrictions applied to mergers made through the acquisition of capital stock, the customary and prevailing method of absorbing competitors at the time the act was passed. It did not apply to asset acquisitions. Thus, as the merger movement of the 1920's reached "fever heights," it was found it could not be checked by antitrust enforcement. 

SHERMAN ACT INEFFECTIVE IN POST-WORLD WAR II MERGER MOVEMENT 

Early in the post-World War II merger movement, the Antitrust Division failed in an attempt to employ section 1 of the Sherman Act to block a merger between United States Steel Corp. and a leading west coast steel fabricator.¹ 10 As the Attorney General's Committee stated, the Columbia Steel decision- *** accented the limitations of section 1 of the Sherman Act in curbing mergers.

This background immediately preceding amended section 7 discloses the apparent congressional objective of establishing more effective rules against mergers.

EFFECTIVENESS OF THE CELLER-KEFAUVER ACT 

Congress amended section 7 of the Clayton Act in 1950 to close the loophole. The effectiveness of amended section 7-the Celler- 

Kefauver Act-in stemming further increases in concentration is indi cated by key cases decided by the courts. The Bethlehem-Youngstown steel merger-the first Government case adjudicated by a district court-made clear that the act applied to mergers threatening to increase concentration in an already highly concentrated industry. 

Judge Weinfeld ruled that the merger offered "an incipient threat of setting into motion a chain reaction of further mergers by other less powerful companies in the steel industry" until "** we reach a point of more intense concentration in an industry already highly concentrated-indeed we head in the direction of triopoly." 12

Three Federal Trade Commission cases passed upon by appellate courts provide additional guidelines. Crown Zellerbach reinforced 

Bethlehem in the application of the anticoncentration approach to regional markets.13 The Spalding case, like Bethlehem, blocked further concentration in an already highly concentrated industry." Reynolds ruled against the intervention by acquisition into a fabricating field by a leading fully integrated producer-supplier on the ground that the effect might be to transform a formerly small business industry into one dominated by powerful, integrated firms.15 

In the first case decided by the U.S. Supreme Court, the Brown 

Shoe case, the Court prevented a merger which may have set into motion a trend toward a level of concentration similar to that which already existed in the steel industry. The Court said: 

Congress was desirous of preventing the formation of further oligopolies with their attendant adverse effects upon local control of industry and upon small business. Where an industry was composed of numerous independent units, Congress appeared anxious to preserve this structure.16

Mere numbers of acquisitions challenged, or cases concluded, are not, of course, indicative of the effectiveness of antitrust enforcement. 

Undoubtedly many mergers have been deterred by antitrust action.17

The scope and thrust of amended section 7, as well as many legal and economic issues are still being tested under the new act. 

Most of the cases brought have been against horizontal mergers. 

Some have involved vertical mergers. Very few have been against mergers of the conglomerate type. According to the National Indus trial Conference Board: 

Horizontal acquisitions have been challenged in 86 percent of the cases (61 percent with horizontal aspects only and 25 percent with both horizontal and vertical aspects.) Challenges to acquisitions which are purely vertical occur in 

10 percent of the cases and challenges to acquisitions which are purely conglomerate occur in 2 percent of the cases. The remaining challenges are to acquisitions with both vertical and conglomerate aspects.18 

Mergers involving a variant of horizontal mergers-so-called market extensions (entry into a new territory through acquisition)—and those of a pure conglomerate type (entry into a new line of activity through acquisition) have yet to be given a rigorous legal test. in the growth of conglomerate corporations, thus, that further research and analysis is needed to determine the impact of mergers on small business.

Antitrust enforcement must, by its very nature, proceed on a caseby-case basis. Thus, patterns of enforcement and the applicability of the law emerge slowly. In the meantime, the public interest is concerned with whether (1) the economic objectives of the law are being achieved, and (2) the repercussions of the merger movement are extending beyond the reach of the antitrust laws to the detriment of small business. It is important, therefore, to look at broad patterns of merger activity-in contrast to the narrow case approach-to determine whether corrective action must be found in programs other than antitrust. 

17 No major integrated steel companies have announced plans to merge since the Bethlehem-Youngstown merger was blocked. Many shoe mergers occurred prior to filing of a case against the Brown-Kinney merger [U.Š. v. Brown Shoe, E.D. Mo. (1959), CCH Trade Cases, par. 69,532, pp. 76, 109-10], but few since. 

In the chain grocery field, according to Mueller and Garoian (pp. 124, 209): "Early in 1959 the Federal 

Trade Commission charged that mergers by Kroger and National Food violated section 7 of the Clayton Act, as well as constituting unfair competition and business practices under the Federal Trade Commission Act. Since these actions were initiated, the extent of merger activity of large chains apparently had decreased considerably (only 1 of the top 10 chains reportedly made any mergers in 1959, and this involved a 2-store firm.-Supermarket News, Jan. 4, 1960, p. 1). The final decisions in these two antitrust cases may have a profound impact on the market structure which emerges in grocery retailing in the next decade."

CHAPTER ACQUISITIONS BY 500 LARGEST INDUSTRIALS AND 50 LARGEST MERCHANDISING FIRMS, 1951–61 

At the end of this section are detailed listings of the merger actions of the top industrial and merchandising firms. Also included is a summary listing of the large industrial firms, indicating the principal kind of business each is engaged in and the number of acquisitions made during the 11-year period under review. These are arranged in groups among the industrial firms: the largest 50; the next 50; the second 100 ranking industrials; and the balance of the list of 500 largest industrial firms.¹ Similarly, a summary listing of the number of acquisitions for the 50 largest merchandising firms is arranged in terms of the 10 largest, the next 10, and the remainder of the 50 largest.2

The detailed listings of acquisitions of the top corporations, it should be emphasized, are based on reports recorded in the financial manuals, supplemented by acquisitions listed in Federal Trade Commission and Antitrust Division complaints. Because of the nature of the sources, it is undoubtedly true that a number of acquisitions were not picked up, and that other acquisitions reported may have been abandoned prior to consummation. Also, where complaints have been brought, the information is considerably more complete. For each of the top industrial and merchandising firms a check has been made against the 1962 Moody's Industrials, to add any acquisitions missed by the Federal Trade Commission in its regular series. 

In general, where antitrust action has been taken against mergers, it has been found that a substantial number of acquisitions were not reported in the financial manuals, indicating that such sources understate the actual number of significant mergers.

The second shows the distribution by 100's, and the last two columns show the distribution by 200's and 250's.

The largest number of mergers was recorded for the third group of 50 companies, and for the second largest group of 100 companies.

Distribution of merger activity by industry

Since the 500 largest industrial companies represent a cross-section of American industry, considerable interest attaches to the industrial distribution of merger activity. Table 8 shows the number of acquisitions of the 500 largest industrials, both by industry and per firm, and reveals two highlights: (1) a few industries have been very active in the merger movement, and (2) in such industries most of the leading firms have made numerous mergers. This suggests a certain degree of rivalry among firms in particular industries for merger opportunities, and at the same time indicates that merger "trends" or "movements" within such industries may have more economic significance than isolated, individual mergers considered apart from industry developments. 

The 500 largest industrials acquired on the average 6.8 companies per firm during the 11-year period. However, 60 of the 500 largest industrials made no acquisitions at all. A total of 227 companies made from 1 to 5 acquisitions; 137 made from 6 to 10 acquisitions; 

54 companies made from 11 to 20 acquisitions; 16, from 21 to 50 acquisitions; 4, from 51 to 100 acquisitions; and 2 made more than 100 acquisitions each.

The 8 most active industries accounted in the aggregate for over 

1,650 acquisitions, representing slightly less than half (48.5 percent) of the total of over 3,400 acquisitions recorded for the 500 largest industrials. These industries were: dairy products, paper and allied products, industrial chemicals, petroleum refining, aerospace, electrical equipment, motor vehicles, and textile-mill products. To focus on certain merger patterns, these eight industries will be considered in some detail below.³ • Some of the other industries where the acquisitions per firm are considerably above the average include paints and varnishes, footwear, lumber and wood products, service industry machines, and communication and electronic equipment.

There are, of course, merger trends outside those involving the very large corporations which have an important impact. For example, during the last 5 years over 25 newspaper mergers have taken place in the United States, in which the daily merged was of some size. Outstanding instances: in Milwaukee, the Sentinel (morning) was acquired by the Journal (afternoon) to establish a one-newspaper city (in terms of ownership); in New Orleans, the Times-Picayune and States merged with the Item to create a onenewspaper city, and then later sold out to the Newhouse newspaper chain.

Dairy products

Clearly, the most merger-prone industry has been dairy products. 

Indeed, mergers in this industry have been under periodic study by the Federal Trade Commission for the past quarter of a century. 

Data cited in the Foremost decision of the Commission indicate that between 1923 and 1961 eight large dairy companies made a total of nearly 2,000 acquisitions.*

The Commission issued reports on acquisitions in the dairy industry in 1937, 1948, and 1955,5 and four Commission complaints against acquisitions of the large dairy companies were filed in 1956.º 

The prominence of the large dairy firms in making acquisitions during the period 1951 through 1961 is indicated by the following data:

1 Ranked 28th among 50 largest merchandising firms.

The economic implications of the merger movement in dairy products, which has continued unabated to the present, were pointed out by the Federal Trade Commission's 1948 merger study: 

*** the chainstore idea of establishing centralized national, or at least regional, control over local units has by no means been confined to the field of retail trade. It has been adopted by many manufacturing industries, particularly by those in which the capital entrance requirements owing to relatively simple technologies are comparatively small and in which the market area is severely limited by the perishability of the products, their high freight costs, or similar factors. In short, this type of merger activity has been most pronounced in those very industries in which technology and market areas are most suited to local, independent, small business operations.

Typically, the firms which have followed this pattern have grown by buying up concerns making the same product in one or a few localities, strengthening their position in those localities by additional acquisitions, branching out to obtain control in other localities, consolidating their local acquisitions into broad regional or district organizations, bringing into the fold leading companies in the major regions, and, by this steady pattern of encroachment, becoming nationwide organizations with a substantial degree of control in the Nation as a whole, a much higher degree in many of the important regions, and a near-monopoly position in numerous individual localities.7 

In the Matter of Foremost Dairies, Inc., Docket No. 6495, Opinion of the Commission, Commissioner 

Elman, dissenting in part (p. 6). 

5 Report of the Federal Trade Commission on the Sale and Distribution of Milk, 75th Cong., 1st sess., H. Doc. No. 95; Report of the Federal Trade Commission on the Merger Movement, 1948; Federal 

Trade Commission Report on Corporate Mergers and Acquisitions, May 1955. 

National Dairy Products Corp., Docket 6651; Borden Co., Docket 6652; Beatrice Foods Co., Docket 6653; 

Foremost Dairies, Inc., Docket 6495. 

7 Report of the Federal Trade Commission on The Merger Movement, 1948, pp. 36-37.

The growth-by-merger process followed by the two leading dairy companies was described as follows: *** the growth of such outstanding nationwide companies as National Dairy

Products Corp. and the Borden Co. could be likened to an acquisition itinerary, sweeping across the country from one large city to another, and gathering in its wake hundreds of companies serving small communities as well. ** *8

It is apparent that the early pattern set by the two leading dairy firms has evolved into an industrywide merger campaign, causing vast inroads in the ranks of small business. 

Most recent acquisitions by dairy concerns have been in the broad dairy field, including milk, ice cream, cheese, and related products. 

They have been both horizontal within similar markets and of the market-extension type. In the Foremost decision the FTC ordered divestiture of nine horizontal and the largest of the market extension acquisitions.

Numerous conglomerate-type acquisitions have also been recorded. 

National Dairy, for example, has moved out of its main line to purchase firms producing jellies and preserves, seeds, citrus fruit juices, and glass containers. It also acquired firms in the substitute product area, producing vegetable oils and margarine. Borden's conglomerate acquisitions included firms turning out preserves, fruits, mushrooms, bouillon cubes, dried soup mixes, sweet and sour red cabbage, Harvard beets, resins, plastic coatings and adhesives, shoe weltings, cosmetics, and processed clams. 

Paper and allied products 8a Mergers in the paper industry where top industrials made 213 acquisitions have been largely vertical, as mills have sought greater pulp resources, or expanded toward the market through acquisition of firms producing various kinds of paper, paperboard, and corrugated boxes. Some 16 leading paper concerns each acquired more than 5 other companies from 1951 through 1961. Six paper companies made more than 10 acquisitions during the 11-year period. 8b Mead Corp., about 60 percent of whose sales comprise white papers and 40 percent liner and corrugating board, was the leader, acquiring over 30 companies.8c Among its acquisitions were a number of manufacturers of various kinds of paper, and several producers of paperboard and containers.

The second most active acquiring firm in the paper field, Standard Packaging Corp., considerably expanded the scope of its operations with 16 acquisitions during the 11-year period. Standard's acquisitions included firms manufacturing cardboard, postal cards, index cards, tag board, and specialties; bottle caps and paper plates; flexible packaging materials for the food industry; paper and metal wrappers, labels, and seals; multigravure labels; paper products and specialties; 

8 Ibid., p. 37. 

8a A key example was the acquisition by Scott Paper Co., a vertically integrated manufacturer of sanitary paper products and household wax paper, of three other companies: Soundview Pulp Co. (bleached sulphite pulp); Detroit Sulphite Pulp & Paper Co. (vertically integrated manufacturer of waxing stock and other papers); and Hollingsworth & Whitney Co. (vertically integrated manufacturer of miscellaneous paper products). These acquisitions were found by the Federal Trade Commission to be in violation of sec. 7 of the Clayton Act, in part because "The timber resources, pulpmills, and other facilities which passed from the acquired companies to Scott, were extensive and diversified and clearly tended to increase the levels of concentration which had prevailed in the sanitary paper products field." The U.S. Court of Appeals for the Third Circuit remanded the case back to the Commission to determine the extent to which Scott's market shares had increased subsequent to the acquisitions. See Scott Paper Co. v. Federal Trade Commission (3d Cir., 1962), opinion of the court filed March 27, 1962. Commission's statement quoted ibid., p. 5.

paper boxes and folding cartons; fine sulfite writing papers; paper, writing papers, and folding box board; paper drinking cups; art calendars, greeting and playing cards; and facial tissues. It also acquired companies manufacturing aluminum, tin, and lead foil, and paper foil closures.

Minnesota Mining & Manufacturing Co., a conglomerate corporation, is included in the "allied" portion of the "paper and allied products" industry. Its principal business consists of tape and allied products (17 percent), coated abrasives, adhesives, coatings, and sealers (12 percent), electrical products (18 percent), graphic products (23 percent), building products (5 percent), chemicals and miscellaneous (6 percent), and foreign (19 percent).8d The company's 15 acquisitions since 1951 similarly take a diverse pattern. Five acquisitions were in the field of electrical insulation products.se Others included firms manufacturing adhesives, glassine and related papers, reinforced plastic aircraft parts (subsequently resold), chemicals, a major broadcasting network, a leading producer of amateur still and movie cameras, projectors, and tape recorders, and a photographic film and related chemicals company. 

The fourth most active of the paper companies racked up 13 acquisitions, including the merger which brought together Union 

Bag & Paper Co. and Camp Manufacturing Co. in 1956, to form

Union Bag-Camp Paper Corp. In a series of mergers, the company acquired others manufacturing container board, corrugated boxes, multiwall paper bags, fiberboard, fiber cases, kraft papers and bags.si 

It also purchased firms in lumber and creosoting, as well as others making facial and cleansing tissues and paper writing tablets. 

Champion Paper & Fibre Co., a fully integrated manufacturer of pulp, paper, and paper products, including printing papers, envelope and tablet papers, and sanitary food boards, made 11 acquisitions, including companies making paper boxes, bottle caps, paraffin cartons, envelopes, school supplies, and stationery. In 1958 the company entered the disposable plastic cup and container field through acquisition of sole licensing rights for product made by Crown Machine & 

Tool Co., Fort Worth, Tex. Two years later it purchased Crown 

Plastic Cup Co., manufacturer of disposable plastic cups. It also purchased a manufacturer of vacuum machinery, and by purchase of two paper product distributors (Carpenter Paper Co. and Whitaker

Paper Co.) became a large wholesale distributor of paper, paper products, stationery products, and allied lines, distributing products produced by others as well as the company.88

The third most active industry was industrial chemicals, which recorded some 204 acquisitions by 25 leading companies-or an average of more than 8 per firm.

10 25 13 23 17 10 14 015363 204 The two acquisition leaders among industrial chemical companies were Olin Mathieson Chemical Corp., which made 25 acquisitions, and Food Machinery & Chemical Corp., which acquired 23 firms during the 11-year period.

Olin Mathieson, as its name suggests, is a product of a major merger in 1954, bringing together Mathieson Chemical Corp. a large producer of both organic and inorganic chemicals and Olin 

Industries, Inc.-concentrated in nonferrous metals and sporting arms and ammunition. The corporate antecedents of each company dated back to the 1890's, and each had expanded largely through earlier mergers. Shortly before combining with Mathieson, Olin purchased a fastener company and a lumber firm. Mathieson numbered other chemical firms, a fertilizer division, and a cotton oil 8h In February 1962 the Antitrust Division filed a sec. 7 action against Kimberly-Clark's acquisition of one of these paper wholesalers, Blake, Moffitt & Towne. The complaint alleged that Kimberly-Clark "is the fourth largest paper producer in the United States *** an integrated producer of printing, writing, book and coated papers *** the world's largest producer of sanitary wadding products; the leading U.S. manufacturer of publication-type coated book paper and one of the five largest manufacturers of all types of book paper; and a major producer of cigarette, condenser, and carbonizing papers. It also produces industrial paper products." It alleged further that "Blake, Moffitt & Towne was the leading independent paper wholesaler in the western part of the United States." The complaint charged that the acquisition may adversely affect competition in the western section of the United States by, inter alia, foreclosing Blake, Moffitt & Towne as a wholesale outlet for other paper manufacturers.

E. R. Squibb & Sons, one of the Nation's best known pharmaceutical houses. The objectives of the Olin merger with Mathieson were outlined as follows: 

Officials indicate that basic reason for merger is to exploit joint interest of both companies in petroleum chemicals and jet fuels and to provide "captive" sources of raw materials for Olin's operations in cellulose and packaging field. Mathieson produces such materials.⁹ 

Olin Mathieson has continued to make numerous acquisitions in a variety of fields: manufacturers of DDT, industrial sodium phosphates, kraft paper, paperboard boxes, and paper bags. It also purchased a wood creosoting company, a producer of aluminum extrusions, a fabricator of aluminum cable and copper wire, an acid firm, and a Canadian firearms company. The range of Olin Mathieson's industrial activities is reflected in its 1961 divisional sales: Chemicals, 25.8 percent; metals, 19.1 percent; organics, 7.9 percent; packaging, 18.9 percent; Squibb (drugs and pharmaceuticals), 17 percent; Winchester- 

Western (sporting arms and ammunition), 11.3 percent.10

A joint venture between Olin Mathieson and Pennsalt Chemicals Corp., to produce sodium chlorate-a chemical used in pulp and paper bleaching and in solid propellant rocket fuels-was challenged in 1961 under the Celler-Kefauver Act and section 1 of the Sherman Act. 

Pennsalt already produced sodium chlorate, and Olin Mathieson was a principal buyer and reseller of the product. The Department of 

Justice alleged that actual and potential competition between them would be eliminated, and that there would be an increase in concentration in the sodium chlorate industry and the allied fields of chlorate and perchlorate chemicals. It was also alleged that Olin Mathieson would be eliminated as an independent customer for sodium chlorate manufactured by competitors of Pennsalt and that suppliers of Olin Mathieson and Pennsalt might be placed at a competitive disadvantage in attempting to furnish materials which either company could sell to the other.

Food Machinery & Chemical Corp., like Olin Mathieson, is a conglomerate corporation, the product of a long series of acquisitions extending back more than 30 years. The company's business is divided into two broad categories machinery and chemicals. In the machinery area, it manufactures garden tractors, power mowers, and chain saws; canning machinery, various types of oilfield machinery and equipment, fruit, vegetable, and egg packinghouse and processing equipment; agricultural, marine, and industrial pumps, and domestic water and sewage disposal systems; agricultural equipment, firefighters, and auto maintenance equipment; packaging and paper box machinery, etc. In the chemical field its products include a long list of industrial chemicals and plastics.

Indeed, the operations of Food Machinery & Chemical Corp. are so diverse that the Fortune 1961 Plant and Product Directory lists the company's products under 41 different product categories. 

During the past 11 years, Food Machinery & Chemical Corp. has acquired other companies making plasticizers and chemicals, bagmaking and packaging machinery, poultry and hog raising equipment, hydrogen peroxide, urea peroxide, potassium persulfate, ammonia

Moody's Industrial Manual, 1954, p. 2324. 

10 Moody's Industrial Manual, 1962, p. 2460.

persulfate, magenesium peroxide, etc., paper box machinery, rotary tillers, power-mowing equipment, sewage treatment and disposal equipment and specialty pumps for building purposes, pesticides, ball bearing swivel joints, rotary hose, transmission assemblies, steam gage processing machinery, wrapping machines, towel-folding machines, oil and gas well equipment, insecticides, seeds, valves, strainers, pipe fittings, special machinery for packaging of frozen items, textile machinery, and automotive brake tools and equipment.

The third most active industrial chemical firm in the current merger movement is Koppers Co., Inc., with 17 acquisitions. Koppers' activities are divided into several categories, including manufacture and sale of crude and refined coal tar products and related chemicals, plastics, dyestuffs, wood preserving, metal products, and gas and coke. 

The company is also engaged in designing and constructing plants used in various phases of the iron and steel industry. 

Koppers' acquisitions during the past decade appear to be largely of horizontal and sometimes vertical character, complementing its particular industrial operations. Acquisitions have included companies processing lubricating oils and greases, making sintering machines and sintering plants, preserving and treating wood, manufacturing printing and container-making machinery, producing mechanical seals for rotating shafts, a strip steel plant, a plastic film manufacturer, a manufacturer of machinery for the corrugated paper box industry, a producer of asphalt paving products, a maker of asphalt emulsion materials, a producer of paving mixtures, and a manufacturer of flexible couplings.¹¹

Petroleum Merger activity in the petroleum industry has been quite extensive, as reflected in two facts: (1) in number of acquisitions the industry was the fourth most prominent, and (2) of the 32 petroleum companies among the Nation's 500 top industrials, all but 5 made acquisitions during the 11-year period. Heading the list in numbers of acquisitions were 2 firms-Continental Oil Co. and Tidewater Oil Co.each acquiring more than 20 other companies. Four others Standard Oil Co. (N.J.), Ohio Oil Co., Kerr-McGee Oil Industries, Inc., and 

Standard Oil Co. (Ind.)-each purchased 10 or more companies.

Several broad tendencies are revealed by petroleum mergers. 

Backward vertical acquisitions were numerous, as refiners sought to increase their crude petroleum holdings.12 Horizontal acquisitions at the refinery level further diminished the ranks, already small, of the independent refiners. Forward vertical acquisitions by major integrated oil companies included purchase of certain tanker terminal operators and wholesale distributors with substantial import oil quotas, the largest being Texaco's acquisition of Paragon Oil Co. 

11 The last named acquisition was ruled in violation of sec. 7 of the Clayton Act, and the acquired company was resold. The court concluded: "Koppers has increased its percentage of the market from 23.0 to 28.8 percent. Thomas has been eliminated as a substantial competitor and the competition heretofore existing between them has been destroyed. Unquestionably, the ability of smaller manufacturers to compete with the combined Koppers-Thomas Co. has been substantially lessened. Koppers' already dominant position in the industry has been enhanced by obtaining Thomas' customer lists which competitors do not 

"It thus appears that one effect of this acquisition, as feared by some of the smaller competitors, has been realized. That is that the entire engineering and manufacturing services of the Koppers organization is now available in the industry for not only the Fast coupling but to promote the Thomas coupling as well. 

Such a result inevitably will squeeze the smaller manufacturers. The total effect of the merger will certainly tend to lessen competition in the coupling industry." United States v. Koppers Company, Inc., 202 F. Supp. 437,449 (W.D. Pa. 1962). 

12 One of the largest was the acquisition by Socony Mobil of the Lake Washington oil and gas field in Louisiana, formerly operated by Freeport Sulphur Co. Another was the joint acquisition of Honolulu Oil 

Co., allegedly the third largest non-integrated U.S. crude oil producer, by Standard (Indiana) and Tidewater, subject of a pending Sec. 7 case.

One of the largest mergers was Standard Oil of California and Standard Oil of Kentucky, reflecting efforts of regionally based majors to secure broader and near-nationwide distribution. Standard of 

California, an integrated oil company with distribution on the west coast and in the Northeast gained entry into the Southeastern part of the country by acquiring Standard of Kentucky, a nonintegrated marketer.12 Standard Oil (New Jersey), with heavy marketing concentration on the east coast sought greater national distribution with a series of acquisitions of Midwest, Florida, and west coast marketers.

Continental Oil also moved toward national distribution through purchase of Midwest and Far West marketers. Gulf, already with nearly nationwide distribution, fortified its position by acquiring additional west coast distributors. In the process of achieving such market extension, the majors have eliminated independent marketers who offered substantial price competition to the integrated majors (notable examples being Douglas Oil Co. of California, acquired by 

Continental Oil Co.; and Wilshire Oil Co. of California, purchased by 

Gulf Oil Corp.). Petroleum companies also have strengthened their position in the production and distribution of natural gas liquids LPG's (including butane, propane, and other hydrocarbon gas liquids) and natural gasolines (blends for motor fuel). This was the objective of the 

Gulf-Warren 13 and Socony-Anchor mergers. 

Other acquisitions are not readily explained: Continental Oil purchased a synthetic detergent plant and a manufacturer of dry chemical fire extinguishers. Standard Oil Co. (New Jersey) acquired a manufacturer of a diversified line of plastic filaments. Kerr-McGee Oil 

Industries acquired two uranium companies. Standard Oil (Indiana) bought a wholesaler of fertilizers and an insurance company. Signal 

Oil & Gas Co. purchased firms making potato chips and peanut butter.

Aerospace Another area of extensive merger activity is the aerospace industry, where 23 firms among the 500 largest industrials acquired 170 other concerns from 1951 through 1961. The changing pattern of defense procurement commencing in the late 1950's, involving a decline. in demand for manned aircraft and a shift to the building of missiles, brought about, in effect, an interindustry merger between aircraft and electronics. This provided a stimulus for companies to enter the aerospace field, and induced old-line aircraft producers to diversify and integrate their product lines and add certain consumer activities to reduce reliance on Government orders. 

As one industrialist put it: *** The Government calls the tune and the missile and electronics programs are subject to wide swings almost daily. These gyrations have and will continue to push many of the smaller electronics contractors into a very deep and very dangerous hole, with the only "out" for many being a consolidation or merger with a source of heavy capital, or a rather bleak profit and loss report.-Paul B. 

Wishart, president, Minneapolis-Honeywell Regulator Co., "What Investor

Should Know in Buying Electronic Stock," Commercial & Financial Chronicle, 

April 21, 1960, page 20. 

12a Potential adverse vertical effects of the Kyso-Socal merger are mitigated somewhat by the final judgment in United States v. Standard Oil Co. (N.J.) (Civil No. 3722, W.D. Ky.) The company is (a) enjoined from obtaining any automotive gasoline, kerosene, heating oil or diesel fuel from Jersey Standard (formerly Kyso's predominant source) for sale in Kyso's former marketing area, (b) directed until July 1, 1976 to obtain, if possible, at least 40 percent of such requirements from independent refiners, and (c) after July 1, 1976, to the extent such requirements are not filled by refineries of Kyso and Socal, secure them, if possible, from independents.

Merger activity was also motivated by efforts to realize greater profits within the corporate structure, rather than to share the business with subcontractors:

Instead of parceling out as much of the multi-hundred-million-dollar contracts *** the large aircraft companies receiving prime contracts tend to keep more and more of the work within their own company. Henry W. Harding, president, Electronics, Inc., editorial, "Weapons System" in Boston Herald, March 31, 1959, page 14. *** more than 70 percent of all missile money goes out from the prime contractors to the subcontractors. And the electronics and telemetry items are the "high markup" items in the business. It made sense *** for the prime contractors to want to keep some of this business under their own roof.-E. E 

Halmos, Jr., op. cit., pages 12-13.

Textron, Inc., long a leading textile producer (whose position had been achieved by an extensive series of mergers), was one "outside" firm that emerged as a member of the aerospace industry by the merger route. About 11 of Textron's 63 acquisitions, 1951-61, fall into the aerospace category, topped by the Bell Defense Group of

Bell Aircraft Corp. (rocket engines, control systems, space techniques, as well as helicopters), Dalmo Victor Co. (electronics, radar antennas, and intercommunication systems), and Nuclear Metals, Inc. (nuclear metallurgical research and prototype production). A decade ago 

Textron was engaged exclusively in various phases of the textile industry. In 1961 its sales structure had been reoriented to this: defense, 29 percent; automotive, 18 percent; consumer goods, 17 percent; industrial, 15 percent; textiles, 15 percent; agrochemical, 6 percent.14

Other particularly active aerospace companies, with the indicated number of acquisitions, were: Thompson Ramo Wooldridge, Inc. (14); 

General Dynamics Corp. (13); Curtiss-Wright Corp. (12); and Ling 

Temco Electronics, Inc. (11). 

Thompson Ramo acquired firms in electronic design and development, jet aircraft components, and chemicals and propellants. It also picked up several companies in high fidelity equipment and com- 14 Moody's Industrials, 1962, p. 1806. Textron's merger activity in the textile field, interestingly, ceased prior to 1957, just as the shift to missiles came about. Prior to that time it had picked up American Woolen 

Co., leader in its field, plus other companies manufacturing cotton goods, upholsterers' supplies, and rayon fabrics.

About 20 of Textron's acquisitions were in metalworking: companies manufacturing such products as screw fasteners and cable terminals; foundry products; metal products and hardware; bathroom fixtures and accessories; die castings and plastic parts for bathroom accessories; controls, filters, and regulators; metalworking machinery; marine engines; steel castings and heavy machinery; automobile body parts and leatherworking machinery; automation equipment for the radio and television industry; heat treating and annealing furnaces; iron and steel forgings; electromechanical equipment; pumps and generators; smalldiameter metal tubing; locknuts and engine nuts; gas meters and regulators; rivets and studs.

Textron's acquisitions also were in various consumer goods fields: a producer of plastic products, brooms, garment and produce bags; a manufacturer of coated materials for handbags and shoes; a unit manufacturing watches and microphones; two manufacturers of optical goods; two producers of men's shoes, and a manufacturer of axles for toys, bolts, burrs, nails, and nuts.

A major conglomerate acquisition made by Textron was Spencer-Kellogg, a large producer of livestock feeds and vegetable oils.

Among Textron's other acquisitions, too numerous to detail, were two plywood firms and two drug manufacturing concerns.
