General Dynamics, "turning out a diversity of products ranging from Atlas missiles to sand and gravel," 15 has had a meteoric rise through mergers to become the "world's biggest manufacturer of weaponry." 16

Formed in 1954 out of a merger between Electric Boat Co. (submarines and powerboats) and Consolidated Vultee 

Aircraft Co., General Dynamics has been the prototype designer and builder of most of the U.S. Navy's nuclear submarines, as well as a leading factor in space vehicles and commercial and military aircraft.¹7

Among its acquisitions were a major producer of radio-TV equipment (Stromberg-Carlson), a number of firms producing various gases, and several producers of concrete materials. 

Curtiss-Wright, an old-line aircraft company, acquired companies in plastics, stainless-steel castings, propulsion research, turbomotors, aerophysics development, missile systems and components, connectors and fittings for the electronic industry, and cameras and stereoscopes. 

Ling-Temco is another aerospace research and developmentsuperpower electronics company to rise in Horatio Alger fashion 18 in less than a decade into the ranks of the Nation's top industrial corporations. Hard on the heels of acquisitions of several smaller electronic firms, Ling merged in 1960 with Temco Aircraft (jet aircraft, missiles, and weapons systems). It took over Altec Electronics (vibration testing systems), an air conditioner firm, and then, in 1961, merged with Chance Vought Aircraft, a ranking aircraft company.19

Electrical equipment 

Proceeding on to the electrical equipment area, one is again impressed by the rapidity with which industry categories are breaking down with the advent of the space age. Firms among the 500 largest industrials classified in electrical equipment according to the elusive symbol of "principal product"-number 20, and this group garnered some 160 acquisitions during the 11-year period, 1951-61. All but three made at least one, and two companies made more than a score of acquisitions. The overall average was eight per company. 

To vividly illustrate the difficulties in industrial classification, the leader among acquiring firms in the electrical equipment "industry" is H. K. Porter Co., Inc., with 33 acquisitions. According to the

Fortune "Plant and Product Directory" (1961), H. K. Porter turns out products under some 74 SIC (Standard Industrial Classification) product codes, ranging from wood file handles to brushes and rollers. 

In addition to such electrical equipment items as transformers, switchgear, fuses, integral and fractional horsepower motors, winding devices, pole-line hardware, and electrical conduit, the company produces electric furnace tool steel, industrial rubber goods, nonferrous metals, various automotive products, paints "for home and industry"-and abroad is a supplier of aircraft engine and airframe accessories (in Scotland), and hydraulic equipment and control systems (in France).20 

15 Fortune, "How a Great Corporation Got Out of Control, The Story of General Dynamics." Pt. I, 

January 1962; pt. II, February 1962, p. 120. 

16 Ibid. General Dynamics is the Nation's top recipient of military prime research and development contracts. See Department of Defense, "500 Contractors Listed According to Net Value of Military Prime Contract Awards for Experimental, Developmental, Test and Research Work, Fiscal Year 1961," p. 1. 

17 Moody's Industrials, 1962, p. 1802. 

18 See "Mergers Play Key Role as James Ling Builds Electronic Empire," Wall Street Journal, May 16, 1960.

20 Moody's Industrials, 1962, p. 851.

H. K. Porter's long list of acquisitions falls in place when reference is made to the foregoing discussion of the company's diverse industrial activities. Thus, in the electrical field it purchased a half-dozen companies, manufacturing such items as complex electrical and electronic devices for aviation; electric equipment for industrial use; electrical porcelain insulators; electric cables and connectors; electric motors; and dry tape, distribution and power transformers. (Note the merger between the electrical industry and aerospace.) Porter also acquired five companies making firebrick and refractory materials, utilized to line furnaces producing steel. It picked up two companies making tool steel and another engaged in producing certain carbon and alloy steel shapes. It acquired companies fabricating steel products, such as wire rope, steel angles, fence posts, and agricultural implements. It purchased a major producer of saws, files, knives, and other tools (Henry Disston & Sons, Inc.). Porter purchased a manufacturer of light rails, switches, and crossings. It purchased four companies producing rubber products-lawn hose, rubber belting, and fluid hose and ducting. In the nonferrous metals area it purchased a company making various types of metal wire, a nonferrous metal fabricator, and a fabricator of metal moldings. Finally, it acquired two paint and lacquer companies.

Other leading acquiring firms in the electrical industry made the following number of acquisitions: McGraw-Edison Co. (21); Westinghouse Electric Corp. (14); Federal Pacific Electric Co. (11); Electric 

Storage Battery Co. (9); and Gould-National Batteries, Inc. (9).

McGraw-Edison's acquisitions were principally in the electrical and cognate fields, including manufacturers of auto heaters and air conditioners, parts for electrical machinery, transformers, porcelain products for electrical and chemical industries, refrigerators, freezers, air conditioners, electrical ranges, small home appliances (irons, toasters, etc.), domestic washers, ironers, dryers, lighting equipment and controls, electrical cooking utensils, ranges and ovens, electrical heaters, armature coils, commercial laundry equipment, and portable electric tools. 

Westinghouse's acquisitions were of various kinds: horizontal (warm-air furnaces, air-conditioning equipment, refrigeration and air-conditioning units for trucks and buses), backward vertical (plywood, presumably for cabinetry), forward vertical (wholesale distribution), and conglomerate (TV stations). 

Federal Pacific Electric's acquisitions were heavily concentrated in electrical switchgear, circuit breakers, and similar fields. Mergers of 

Electric Storage Battery and Gould-National Batteries were chiefly horizontal within the battery business. 

Motor vehicles and equipment

Mergers in the motor vehicles and equipment industry over a long period of years have reflected efforts of parts and component suppliers of large auto firms to diversify and escape dependence on serving their large customers, themselves highly integrated. The big automobile companies, meanwhile, have made selective purchases of firms in other fields. In all, the 20 firms (among the 500 largest industrials) in the industry acquired 139 companies from 1951 to 1961, or 7 per company on the average.

The most acquisitive of the group was Borg-Warner Corp., a conglomerate organization that purchased 15 other companies during the 11 years-ranging into such fields as aluminum insulation, power-

saws, aircraft controls, loaders and pulverizing equipment, centrifugal turbine and pumping equipment, and air conditioning. 

Studebaker and Packard joined forces in 1954, and the hyphenated firm proceeded to buy an aerophysics development company, two phonograph record makers, a firm making delivery vehicles, buses and ambulances, two firms in the plastics business (one in research), farm equipment manufacturing facilities, a sizable auto radiator and air-conditioning firm (Fedders Corp.), a manufacturer of electrical generators, a producer of soil and pressure pipe, and a company making motor oil additives.

While the leading automobile manufacturers did not make a large number of acquisitions, several were highly significant. General 

Motors early in the 1950's bought up a fire engine producer, and a major factor in roadbuilding machinery.21 Ford bought out two farm implements firms, purchased the sparkplug and battery business of Electric Auto-Lite Co.,22 and merged Philco Corp., one of the

Nation's leading manufacturers of TV and radio sets, refrigerators, freezers, and electronic equipment, with 12 plants in the United States and 4 in foreign countries. Ford officials outlined the objectives of the Philco acquisition in these words: *** to broaden operations, to provide entry into new fields, and to make possible a fuller participation by Ford in the national defense and space effort.23 

Textile mill products 

Throughout the post-World War II years merger activity has been especially marked in the textile industry. Leading textile firms have been involved in both horizontal and vertical mergers, and the leading participants in the merger movement have reached out to consolidate various phases of the textile industry under common ownership. A total of 110 acquisitions were made by textile leaders among the top industrial firms during the 11-year period. 24 The above figures represent an understatement inasmuch as Textron, Inc., as noted above, has shifted into the aerospace industry. Were its 63 acquisitions to be added to the textile mill products list, the total of 173 would make it the fifth most active industry in terms of number of acquisitions during the 11-year period.

A study made by the Cotton Textile Institute indicated that in the years 1940-46 about one-fifth of the production facilities of the cotton textile industry changed hands. Basic producers of gray goods were expanding forward into the finishing operations, while various fabricators were moving backward into the gray-goods field. 

Half of the spindle facilities sold changed hands because of vertical acquisitions.25

The most active firms in that earlier phase of the merger movement were Burlington Mills (originally a gray-goods manufacturer which moved forward into the finishing field), M. Lowenstein & Sons (prominent New York converter that expanded backward by acquiring gray-goods mills), and J. P. Stevens & Co., Inc., (a leading sales agent which expanded backward vertically by acquiring gray-goods mills). 

During the 1950's, Burlington continued to lead in the acquisition movement. In contrast to Textron (discussed above), however, Burlington's acquisitions were confined to the textile and apparel fields. It picked up companies in dyeing and bleaching, converting, hosiery manufacturers, producers of woolens, blankets, cotton, rayon, and glass fabrics, velvets, and carpets and rugs. Among its acquisitions were some of the industry's most prominent names-Goodall- 

Sanford, Pacific Mills, Ely & Walker Dry Goods.26 

J. P. Stevens also has continued to make acquisitions in the textile and apparel business. Its acquisitions included firms making sheets and pillow cases, textiles, broad goods, velvets and upholstery fabrics, covert cloth, melton, woolens and worsteds, taffetas, satins, rayon and nylon, and plastic filaments. Prominent among its acquisitions were such names as Cheney Bros., Worumbo Manufacturing Co., and Forstmann Woolen Co.

Kayser-Roth, a fully integrated manufacturer and seller of hosiery and knitwear, was also quite active, making acquisitions of firms in its own particular area, including manufacturers of swimsuits, sweaters, hosiery, lingerie, girdles, and garters.

Indian Head Mills, another diversified manufacturer and processor of textile products, purchased companies engaged in cotton textile manufacture, yarn dyeing, threads, twine, cordage and yarn, cotton and rayon fabrics, broad silks, rayon and nylon piece goods, and bleaching, dyeing, printing, and finishing of cotton.

The continued merger activity in the textile business gives further support to a prediction made a decade and a half ago by one of the industry's leading trade journals, Textile World:

Belief is prevalent that the industry is entering an era of larger mill groups and that consequently fewer men will control the majority of its equipment and its products. Some extremists even forecast that the time is coming when a mere five or six companies will dominate the textile field just as has come to pass in the automobile industry.27

Other leaders in the merger movement

Finally, reference should be made to the handful of other luminaries in the merger movement, including the following:

Company

Number of acquisitions, 1951-61 73 35 21 205 Dramatizing the pace of the merger movement, American-Marietta, the 127th ranking industrial corporation, joined forces with the Martin 

Co., No. 68, marking the apogee of a series of some 73 mergers during the decade. Eleven years ago, American-Marietta was merely a manufacturer and distributor of paints, enamels, varnishes, lacquers, and kindred items. Today, Martin-Marietta Corp. is divided into three major corporate groups; namely, construction materials, chemicals, and aerospace.

American-Marietta's acquisitions in the early 1950's were largely in the paint and lacquer field. It then proceeded to make scores of acquisitions of companies in various areas of the cement and concrete pipe business. In January 1961, the Federal Trade Commission filed a Clayton Act section 7 case challenging these acquisitions and alleging that American-Marietta had become one of the largest U.S. manufacturers of concrete pipe.28

Genesco, Inc. (formerly General Shoe Corp.) acquired a number of other prominent shoe companies, including Nisley Shoe, Johnston & Murphy, Whitehouse & Hardy (operating both shoestores and men's clothing stores), I. Miller & Sons, and Delman, Inc., a manufacturer and retailer of ladies' handmade shoes. 28a It has also acquired manufacturers of men's clothing, lingerie, perfumes and men's toiletries, and pajamas and nightwear. 

Litton Industries' 27 acquisitions were in many fields: electronics, office machines, oilfield exploration, shoe machinery, aerial topography, nuclear submarines, and trading stamps.

Georgia-Pacific made 25 acquisitions, confined mostly to the timber, lumber, and wood products field, but some of forward integration type, such as corrugated shipping containers, grocery bags and sacks, and the wholesale distribution of building materials. 

Ekco Products Co. made a long list of acquisitions in an almost equally long list of different fields: household copper cleaner, cellulose sponge products, enamel housewares, tea strainers, disappearing 

28 See apps. A-C. 

28a The last named acquisition was challenged by the Antitrust Division under sec. 7 of the Clayton Act, and a consent decree was entered in 1956, requiring limited divestiture.

clotheslines, food-handling equipment for retail meat markets, 28b industrial fluorescent lighting fixtures, bottle cap openers, rolling pins and kitchen boards, and glass fiber fishing rods. 

Universal Match Co. made acquisitions in such fields as cooking apparatus and heating stoves, vending machines, armaments, air circulating systems, refrigerators, and electromechanical systems.

ACQUISITIONS OF 50 LARGEST MERCHANDISING FIRMS 

During the 11-year period, 1951-61, the 50 largest merchandising firms acquired a total of 332 other companies. 

Concentration of merger activity by size groups among top merchandising firms As in the case of the industrials, the biggest incidence of merger activity appears to be concentrated in the larger firms. As shown in table 13, the 10 largest merchandisers acquired 85 firms, while the third largest group of 10 acquired 87. The 20 largest acquired 162, as compared with 119 for the next 20. The largest 25 merchandising firms acquired 214, as against 118 for the next 25.

The Nation's largest grocery chain, the Great Atlantic & Pacific Tea 

Co., has been conspicuously inactive in the merger movement, with only two acquisitions recorded. These did not involve acquisitions of other grocery chains, but were vertical in character, one being the purchase of a nut company and the other a dairy products firm. 

Safeway Stores an organization which owes much of its early growth to mergers 29-made some 26 acquisitions during the past 11 years. These were concentrated chiefly in the retail area, and ranged to many sections of the country, including New Mexico, Missouri, Washington, Oregon, Oklahoma, Arizona, Texas, Kansas, California, Iowa, Nevada, Nebraska, Colorado, and Wyoming. Safeway sold out its New York division of supermarkets to another large chain, First National Stores. None of Safeway's acquisitions have been challenged by the antitrust agencies. 

Acquisitions of Kroger, National Tea, and Grand Union, on the other hand, are the subject of actions now pending before the Federal 

Trade Commission.30 Kroger, the Nation's third largest grocery chain, acquired supermarkets in Texas, Wisconsin, and Louisiana, which are under litigation; it also acquired stores in the District of 

Columbia and Illinois. In addition, it moved outside its main line to purchase a chain of New Jersey drugstores.31 National Tea's challenged acquisitions included numerous supermarkets located in 

Michigan, Tennessee, Illinois, Missouri, Iowa, Colorado, Minnesota,

Louisiana, and Alabama. It also acquired a bread and bakery products firm in Wisconsin. Grand Union merged supermarkets in New 

Jersey, the District of Columbia, Connecticut, Virginia, Florida, Maryland, New York, and Puerto Rico. Its supermarket acquisitions in Schenectady, N.Y., and Long Island, N.Y., are under litigation before the Federal Trade Commission.

Detailed studies 32 of the recent merger movement in food retailing reach the following conclusions: (1) Retail grocery merger activity was minimal during the first phase of the current merger movement, but stepped up sharply in the 1950's, to reach peaks-both in number of stores acquired and in sales of acquired stores-in 1955 and 1958; 33 (2) merger activity of the 20 largest chains was a principal cause of an increase in concentration both on a national and a local basis.34 

Many of the acquisitions made by the large chains have been of the market extension variety. In other words, they mark the entry (by

While such mergers may not eliminate competition between rivals in the same immediate area, they contribute to the broader tendency of the large chains to expand apace, not by internal growth, but by merger. Moreover, there seems to be a mushrooming tendency for local chains to merge as a preliminary step to eventual sale to larger regional or national chains.35 

The larger chains-like the big dairy firms-often pick off leading local enterprises.36

Food chain acquisitions have not been solely horizontal. Vertical acquisitions have been part of a movement by grocery chains dating back a half century. Chains now manufacture substantial proportions of the baked goods, coffee, and concentrated milk they sell, as well as fairly significant percentages of such products as meat, dairy products other than concentrated milk, canned products, and processed poultry products.37

In the wholesale food field, Consolidated Foods made some 29 acquisitions during 1951-61. Consolidated is engaged in the canning, manufacturing, processing, and packaging of a wide line of food products, carbonated soft drinks, canned beverages and fruit flavors, and also conducts a wholesale food distribution business and operates three retail food chains and one retail drug chain. Its acquisitions were primarily in food processing. One of its mergers, involving purchase of a California manufacturer of dried food seasonings, is the subject of a case pending before the Federal Trade Commission. Consolidated also bought out a chain of drugstores in Iowa.

General merchandise and department stores Leading general merchandise retailers have participated in the merger movement. Sears, Roebuck made vertical acquisitions of suppliers of record players, tape recorders, and loud speakers; wallpaper and latex paints; air conditioners, vaccuum cleaners, and food mixers; and electronic equipment. J. C. Penney purchased a mail order business. Montgomery Ward acquired a supplier of paint and two companies operating department stores. 

The department store business has also witnessed considerable merger activity, and more and more it appears that local units are becoming affiliated with regional or national groups. 

35 For example, Alpha-Beta Food Markets, Inc., a sizable grocery chain in the Los Angeles area, in 1958 acquired the 13-store Raisin Markets, Inc., also of Los Angeles, then in 1960 sold out to American Stores Co., the Nation's fourth ranking chain. The merger represented American's first intervention in the Los Angeles area. See "FTC Staff Report on Economic Inquiry into Food Marketing," p. 137. 

36 Safeway, for example, in 1958 bought out Thriftway Stores, Inc., of Des Moines, a leading Iowa chain. 

37 Mueller & Garoian, p. 125. "*** The most significant change in vertical integration by chains in the past 30 years is that more chains have become integrated and chains have entered more fields of grocery manufacturing." "The reason chains are able to integrate into so many fields is that they are in the unique position of being able to overcome the main barrier which makes entry difficult or impossible for other firms; that is, the product differentiation barrier. As chains become large they are able to develop consumer acceptance of their own brands. And if they become large enough to sell the output of an efficiently sized plant under their own brand, they are in the position of being able to integrate into such industries by either buying a going concern or by building their own plant. Thus the main determinants of whether a chain can integrate into a particular field are (1) the development of its own brand, and (2) the expansion to a size adequate to operate and utilize the output of efficiently sized plants manufacturing various products." Ibid., pp. 125-126.

2 

THE TREND TOWARD SUPERCONCENTRATION 

To sum up, the merger movement of the 1950's and 1960's poses many problems for small business. During a period when the number of businesses has failed to keep pace with the growth in the economy as a whole, serious inroads are being made into the ranks of medium-sized and small business by the merger movement which tends to siphon off the more successful firms. The merger movement has pushed into postwar heights and affected a wide spectrum of industries with dairy products, paper, chemicals, petroleum, aerospace-electronics, motors, textiles, and chain food distribution most prominent.

In each industrial sphere merger activity appears to be rooted in peculiar characteristics of structural development. Thus, in dairy products, the pattern of achieving national, or at least regional, domination by a few huge dairy organizations has continued through the merger process in a cross-country sweep. The paper industry has witnessed a series of vertical mergers, as mills have sought greater pulp resources, or expanded toward the market by acquiring firms producing various kinds of paper, paperboard, and corrugated boxes. 

Chemical companies have joined forces in order to exploit joint interests and garner "captive" sources of raw materials. Merger activity in petroleum has reflected efforts of major refiners to expand their crude petroleum holdings, to pick off some of the few remaining independent refiners, and to acquire certain tanker terminal operators and wholesale distributors with substantial import oil quotas. Most important has been the drive of regionally based major oil companies to secure new sales areas and near-nationwide distribution, a process of market extension achieved at the cost of elimination by the majors of substantial price competition from independent marketers. Petroleum companies have also attempted through mergers to strengthen their position in the production and distribution of natural gas liquids. 

The changing pattern of defense procurement, subordinating manned aircraft to missiles, has, in effect, brought an interindustry merger between aircraft and electronics. Smaller electronics firms have been squeezed by the vicissitudes of Government procurement and sought alliances with the well-entrenched big firms, who, in turn, have been anxious to pick up needed technical know-how, scarce scientists, and engineers. Old-line aircraft companies have merged with electronics firms to supply the modern weaponry, and have at the same time attempted to achieve greater diversification through the acquisition of more consumer type activities.

The revolution in industrial techniques, marking the advent of the space age, has influenced the swashbuckling rise-through massive mergers of some new factors in the ranks of the top industrial corporations, but the older dominant firms have not been idle, themselves making significant, selective, and huge mergers. Of topical interest is the emergence of a major merger-built textile corporation-again through mergers-as a member of the aerospace industry, while textile companies continue to fuel a merger movement of massive proportions--horizontal and vertical-in their own sphere. Meanwhile,

pressures for the expansion in living space-the mass migration of population to the suburbs have brought in train a revived merger movement among retail food chains, accentuating national and local concentration, with a mushrooming tendency for local chains to merge preliminary to sale to larger regional or national chains.

Cutting across industry lines indeed, breaking down traditional industry categories-the face and form of the current merger movement is to be found in the pervasive rise of the conglomerate corporation. This is the new challenge to small business the threat to its potentialities for entry, growth, and survival. The impact of the conglomerate corporation on small business is often found in its side effects, rather than in immediate repercussions. The conglomerate corporation possesses advantages in magnified form over smaller rivals. 

The entrenched horizontal corporation may exercise competitionlessening control over particular product markets. Sitting astride a number of local or regional markets, the horizontal company with a full line may have even greater market power. Vertical control by large corporations over the various stages of the flow of product also places formidable barriers in the path of the small, nonintegrated firms seeking to compete. 

The conglomerate corporation, however, often has the advantages of the horizontal and vertical firms, and more. It can weather a storm of competition or emerging technological obsolescence in one of its areas of interest, while seeking opportunities in other newly developing fields. It has staying power, ready access to the capital markets, accessibility to new Government research and development grants, and supply contract awards.

In the merger report which formed an important part of the legislative history to the Celler-Kefauver Act, the Federal Trade Commission 

***the motives underlying conglomerate acquisitions appear to include such diverse incentives as desires to spread risks, to invest large sums of idle liquid capital, to add products which can be handled with existing sales and distribution personnel, to increase the number of products which can be grouped together in the company's advertisements, etc.

But in addition to these factors, there is present in most conglomerate acquisitions a simple drive to obtain greater economic power. With the economic power which it secures through its operations in many diverse fields, the giant conglomerate corporation may attain an almost impregnable economic position. Threatened with competition in any one of its various activities, it may sell below cost in that field, offsetting its losses through profits made in its other lines-a practice which is frequently explained as one of meeting competition. The conglomerate corporation is thus in a position to strike out with great force against smaller business in a variety of different industries. As the Commission has previously pointed out, there are few greater dangers to small business than the continued growth of the conglomerate corporation.38 

The antitrust laws, which failed to stem the horizontal and vertical merger movements of the 1890's and the 1920's, have had no visible deterrent effect on the conglomerate merger movement of the 1950's and 1960's. History indeed reveals a striking cultural lag in the fashioning of restraints on the growth of concentrated economic 

38 Federal Trade Commission Report on the Merger Movement (1948), p. 59.

power. The ranks of large business,39 as well as small business, are under pressure from the unabated trend toward superconcentration.
